> **Implements Pillar**: The World Never Lies (P1 — no mask, vignette, fog or full-frame gamma between a lit pixel and the screen); Dread Before Chaos and Sanity Rewrites Reality (P2/P4 — no uncaused pixel motion, so tremor and glide stay readable and the frame is an anchor the Filter cannot move); The Web Watches (P3 — the camera never reads world state, and the web's drift stays readable on a grid that does not crawl)

## Summary

The Pixel-Perfect Render Pipeline & Camera is the surface every visual system draws onto and the window the player sees through: a 640×360 logical world scaled by a whole number to the display, rendered with nearest sampling and pixel snapping so no pixel ever moves without a cause in the world; a HUD on its own layer that world light, the sanity filter and camera motion can never touch; a draw-order contract that makes a 3/4-oblique ship read correctly; and a camera that is a window, not a sensor — it follows the player and leans where their beam points, responds to nothing the world does, holds still when the ship does, and cuts rather than pans. It exists because the art bible's identity (*What You Light, You Can Read*) and three pillars rest on an image that is faithful to the simulation at the scale of one art pixel, and because the lighting-budget spike that decides the renderer's cost cannot run without the pipeline it measures.

> **Quick reference** — Layer: `Foundation` · Priority: `MVP` · Key deps: `None` upstream (contracts: Command & Simulation Layer CR-10/11; art bible 8.1–8.4, 7.0); 17 downstream dependents *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*

## Overview

The Pixel-Perfect Render Pipeline & Camera is the surface every visual system of The Synaptic Sea draws onto, and the window through which the player sees it. It fixes a **logical resolution of 640×360** scaled by an integer to the player's display (3× at 1080p, 4× at 1440p, 6× at 2160p, 2× on a Steam Deck), renders the world into its own viewport with nearest-neighbour sampling and pixel snapping so that every art pixel is a crisp square of screen pixels, keeps the HUD on a separate root-viewport layer that the world's glow, ambient and occlusion can never touch, and owns the **camera**: what it follows, how it moves, where it may not go, and the integer zoom steps it may take. It also owns the draw-order contract that makes a 3/4-oblique ship read correctly — floors never sort, wall faces and actors sort together by Y, caps draw above actors — and the single full-screen hook point where the Perception Filter is allowed to corrupt what a low-sanity player sees, below the HUD and never touching it.

The player never interacts with this system directly. They experience it as an image that is faithful: the one-pixel wander of a lying needle and the two-pixel identity bands on a crewmate's helmet stay legible because nothing ever shimmers or blurs; the edge of their own light is drawn as Lighting computes it, never softened or extended by a mask; the frame holds still when the ship does, and moves only as the simulation moves.

It exists because the art bible's whole identity rests on it: *What You Light, You Can Read* needs true 2D occlusion (not a vignette), legibility at one art pixel, and a HUD exempt from world light (art bible Principle 1, 7.0, 8.1–8.4), and the lighting-budget spike that decides the renderer's cost (8.12) cannot run without the pipeline it measures. Seventeen systems, across sixteen rows of the Interactions table (Settings & Input Rebinding and Accessibility Settings share one row — see Dependencies), rest on it directly, and every presentation system inherits its contract *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*: subscribe to the kernel as `PRESENTATION`, read last-committed state, interpolate with `tick_alpha`, write only what is drawn (Command & Simulation Layer CR-10, CR-11). Without it, the game would still simulate correctly and look wrong — which, for a game whose pillar is *The World Never Lies*, is the same as being wrong.

**Design-level only.** `SubViewport` vs root rendering, stretch and snap settings, whether lights render at base or native resolution (the spike's pass A/B), `Camera2D` smoothing vs manual interpolation — these are implementation choices → **becomes an ADR** (render pipeline / tick-source ADRs). *(Framing: technical infrastructure, no ADR yet to cite, fantasy felt indirectly — the recommended options for a `Core`/`Foundation` system under `autonomous` automation.)*

## Player Fantasy

*Indirect — the player never meets this system; they meet a stillness it keeps.*

**When the ship is still, the screen is still.** The game teaches the player to read motion as evidence. A needle that shakes a pixel is a stressed hull warning them. A needle that glides slowly with nothing in the ship to explain it is lying. The four trim bands on a crewmate's helmet are who that is. All of this lives at the scale of one or two art pixels, so it only works if no pixel ever moves for a reason that isn't in the world: no shimmer from a non-integer scale, no camera crawl from rounding, no actor jittering a pixel against the floor as the camera follows. Every pixel that changes was changed by something in the world.

The anchor moment is on the first sortie. The player stands in a dead scrubber bay with their beam on a wall gauge. They stop walking and the frame stops with them. Then the needle moves. Because nothing else on screen moved, the player knows the motion belongs to the ship, or to their own mind, and not to the screen. They lean in to see whether it shakes or glides, and that judgement is the game. The comfort is structural: *if I saw it move, something moved it.*

**Pillars served.** *Dread Before Chaos*: "Every breach is preceded by a tell a careful player could have perceived (sound, light, gauge drift, hull stress)" — gauge drift is perceivable only if render noise can neither fake it nor hide it. *Sanity Rewrites Reality*: "the lie extends from the gauge to the sprite" — the art bible's settle/tremor/glide grammar (7.3) is how a player tells the lie from the warning, and a pipeline that adds its own motion corrupts both. *The World Never Lies*: the rendering stack is neither instrument nor perception, so it may not be wrong either. *The Web Watches*: filament movement at ≤ 1 art px/s (art bible 2.11) is readable only on a grid that does not crawl.

**Design tests.**
- If we are debating a presentation effect that moves world pixels with no cause in the world (sub-pixel smoothing, eased camera lag that overshoots, non-integer zoom, a softening filter, screen shake) against holding the grid: hold the grid, or move the effect into the world (a needle, a sprite, a light).
- The fantasy has failed if a player can see a one-art-pixel movement that no system put there. Two cases count: a static scene, and a followed actor that shifts relative to the floor while the camera tracks it. That is a counterfeit tremor or a counterfeit glide — a Pillar 2 and Pillar 4 violation, not a cosmetic bug. It is testable on day one with a frame-diff: hold the sim with no committed change and the camera target still, and the only world pixels allowed to differ between frames are those owned by a declared animating source (flicker, scrolling medium, web drift); then walk an actor across a static room, and the actor-to-floor offset may change only by whole art pixels in step with committed position.

*What this section does not claim.* The pipeline does not make the world honest; the kernel does. It does not make motion meaningful; the art bible's grammar does. It does not make a reading true; the HUD frame stays put and undimmed (HR-1), but the needle inside it may lie under class (b). It does not own the edge of the light — Lighting and Principle 1 do; the pipeline's part is only that no mask, vignette, fog layer or edge-darkening ever sits between a lit world pixel and the screen, which is a prohibition in Core Rules, not a fantasy. The image is up to one tick behind committed state and never ahead of it (kernel `tick_alpha` clamps at 1). Under lighting pass A the cone edge is a 3-px stair-step at 1080p, and this section does not promise a smooth one. *(Alternatives considered: "the edge belongs to the light" — evocative but credits the pipeline with Lighting's guarantee; "a porthole, not a lens" — accurate camera policy, but policy is not a moment the player feels.)*

**Downstream implications carried into Section C and Game Feel.** Any whole-frame shake counterfeits a tremor, the honest Tell of 2.7: hull stress goes into real needles, dust direction and sprites, not into the frame, and whether any shake survives (e.g. breach impact after the tell has landed) is decided explicitly against design test 1. The commonest way this fantasy fails is the followed actor and the world rounding to different integers in the same frame, so QA tests motion during follow, not only a still scene. The HUD exemption is anchoring, not truth.

## Detailed Design

### Core Rules

*Specialists consulted: systems-designer (primary), engine-programmer, gameplay-programmer, game-designer, ux-designer. Disagreements resolved and logged 2026-10-26; items the specialists could not resolve are in Open Questions.*


**Vocabulary.** *Base px* (*art px*): one pixel of the 640×360 logical grid. *Screen px*: one pixel of the window. *k*: the world's integer scale (screen px per base px). *k_base*: the effective base scale — the manual `k` when Settings sets one below Auto, else the automatic `k` (C6, D.5) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. *H*: the HUD's integer scale, independent of k. *Picture*: the region of the window that shows the game (the window unless letterboxed). *World viewport*: the `SubViewport` holding every lit thing; its canvas **is the current body's frame** (PR-6 C2). *Body*: a Ship Body & Interior Physics object with its own reference frame (home-ship, derelict, pod, detached section). *Sort origin*: the base-px row an object sorts by on the Y-sorted layer. *Pose*: the camera's output — integer base-px position in the body frame plus the current k. *`present_time`*: the pipeline's render-rate clock (PR-9). *Tick*, *`tick_alpha`*, *read view*, *`PRESENTATION`*: as the kernel GDD defines them (CR-10, CR-11, D.3).

---

**PR-1. Logical resolution and integer scale.** The world is authored at **640×360 base px** (registry: `BASE_RESOLUTION`) (art bible 8.1, decided) and drawn at an integer scale `k = floor(min(W / 640, H / 360))` where `W × H` is the window's **pixel** size — never derived from DPI; `content_scale_factor` stays 1.0 and `allow_hidpi` behaviour on the Deck and on Windows ≥ 125 % scaling is logged by spike step 11 (→ D for the variable table). The **minimum window is 1280×720** (registry: `MIN_WINDOW`) (C-R; `Window.min_size` `[BIN]`), so `k ≥ 2` on every display. Nothing in the world is ever drawn at a non-integer scale; the only non-integer text anywhere is the vector reading face on the chart-table layer (7.1). At every window size the player sees square, unblurred base pixels; the trade is the picture size, never pixel integrity. The player may choose k below the automatic value in Settings ("integer world scale: Auto + list", PR-14) — see PR-2 for why a manual k forces `keep`.

| Window (screen px) | k | `keep`: picture and bars | `expand`: logical size (base px) | Tiles in view (expand) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1280×720 | 2 | 1280×720, none | 640×360 | 13.3 × 7.5 |
| **1280×800 (Steam Deck)** | 2 | 1280×720, 40 top + 40 bottom | 640×400 | 13.3 × 8.3 |
| 1366×768 | 2 | 1280×720, 43/43 L/R, 24/24 T/B | 682×384 (even; 1/1 residual) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)* | 14.2 × 8.0 |
| 1365×768 (odd width) | 2 | 1280×720, 42/43 L/R, 24/24 T/B | 682×384 (the logical size is always even, so the engine never sees an odd or fractional base; the 1 px residual is letterboxed, 0/1 — what the engine draws in it is step 11's) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)* | 14.2 × 8.0 |
| 1600×900 | 2 | 1280×720, 160/160 L/R, 90/90 T/B (36 % of the screen black) | 800×450 | 16.7 × 9.4 |
| **1920×1080** | 3 | none | 640×360 | 13.3 × 7.5 |
| 1920×1200 | 3 | 1920×1080, 60/60 T/B | 640×400 | 13.3 × 8.3 |
| **2560×1440** | 4 | none | 640×360 | 13.3 × 7.5 |
| 2560×1080 (21:9) | 3 | 1920×1080, 320/320 L/R | 852×360 (even; 2/2 residual) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)* | 17.8 × 7.5 |
| **3440×1440 (ultrawide)** | 4 | 2560×1440, 440/440 L/R | 860×360 | 17.9 × 7.5 |
| **3840×2160** | 6 | none | 640×360 | 13.3 × 7.5 |

*Rationale.* 640×360 is the only common base that lands on an integer at 720p, 1080p, 1440p and 2160p (8.1's table), so the four sizes the bible calls common need no policy at all; PR-2 exists for everything else. *Alternative rejected:* fractional scaling with a sharpening filter — it breaks 8.0's single grid and makes the 1 px contour, the 2 px bands and the 2 px fringe uneven widths, which 5.6's minimum-feature rule forbids. *Engine fact that makes this rule load-bearing:* `project.godot` today has no `[display]` block and no `rendering/textures` or `rendering/2d/snap` keys, so the project currently runs at the engine built-ins — 1152×648, stretch `disabled`, `fractional`, LINEAR-filtered, unsnapped `[BIN]` — and looks "almost right" in the editor at 1×. PR-15 is the guard.

---

**PR-2. Aspect: `keep` for the spike, `expand` as the IDEAL, both bounded; bars are true black.**

- **`keep` (letterbox).** The picture is `640k × 360k`, centred; the remainder is bars. This is the spike architecture (8.4) and the **default until 8.12 step 2 and step 11 have run**, because the world viewport is a fixed 640×360 and nothing resizes.
- **`expand` (IDEAL, 8.1/8.10, post-spike).** The world viewport's logical size becomes `(2·floor(W/(2k)), 2·floor(H/(2k)))` (always even; D.3), clamped to **≤ 960×540** at default zoom (PROVISIONAL cap = 1.5× base; → D), remainder letterboxed; the base size and k both stay integer at every window (C-R); the **HUD rect is pinned to the `k_base` picture rect** (never the window, never the zoomed picture), so the HUD does not move on a zoom step even when a ladder field leaves a few black pixels inside it (D.5) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. The camera stays centred on the same target, so an ultrawide player sees more void left and right and a Deck player 20 more rows above and below.
- **Manual k forces `keep`.** A player-chosen k below the automatic value under `expand` would be a permanently wider frame (at 1080p, k = 2 gives 960×540 — exactly the zoom-out view, as the default), which the game-designer's decision forbids ("never a wider default frame"; the frame is changed only by the player's `zoom_in` / `zoom_out` steps, PR-6 C6 *(Amended 2026-10-26 — consistency check)*). So when `k < k_auto`, aspect is `keep` regardless of the setting. *(Systems-designer resolution of a conflict between the Settings list and the camera decision — flagged for the ux-designer and game-designer to confirm.)*

*The honesty caveat on "more void, not more information" (8.1).* The claim holds in the dark states (2.4–2.8), where everything outside the beam is black whichever width the frame is. It does **not** fully hold in two cases: (i) at 3440×1440 under `expand` the half-width is ≈ 9 tiles, so the narrow cone's 8-tile reach (8.3) fits entirely on screen where a 16:9 player's is clipped at 6.7 tiles; (ii) in the home-ship stable state (2.3) every fixture is on and a wider frame shows more lit ship. A 1600×900 player under `expand` sees 25 % more in each axis than a 1080p player. The cap bounds the gap; it does not remove it. **Routed to creative-director** with the recommendation to accept `expand` (a third of the screen black at 1600×900 is a worse experience than a slightly wider frame in a co-op horror game with no competitive stake) and to record the gap in Edge Cases. Second cost: under pass B (PR-7) light is paid per lit screen pixel — 4.95 M px at 3440×1440 against 2.07 M at 1080p; under pass A the cost grows only to 860×360 base px. The spike measures pass B at 1080p only; `expand` under pass B is a post-spike measurement.

*Bars.* Letterbox bars are **true black, outside the world `SubViewport`**, untouched by the Perception Filter pass and by any camera jolt (C-R). Because the void inside the world is also near-black (0–1 % ambient, 2.5), a bar could be mistaken for unlit hull: the ux-designer recommends a **1-HUD-px Graphite hairline at the picture edge** — routed to art-director (it is a UI mark, 4.6 palette). What the engine actually draws in the remainder under `keep` is NOT SOURCEABLE (step 11).

*Deck safe area (PROVISIONAL).* The HUD is authored **anchor-relative to the visible picture rect**, never the window, with the 7.0 safe margin of 5 % of the picture rounded up to whole HUD px per axis (registry `safe_margin`, the HUD & Suit Layer's formula): 32 × 18 HUD px at 1920×1080, H 3; 22 × 14 HUD px at 1280×800, H 3 *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from hud-and-suit-layer)* — and must survive a fractional HUD canvas (the picture divided by H is not always integer) and both aspects (C-R). The Deck has no notch; the margin is ergonomic and the ux-designer may raise it per device. Deck *text* size is PR-12's problem.

*Fullscreen.* Default `MODE_FULLSCREEN` (borderless window covering the screen, `Window.mode = 3` `[BIN]`) rather than `EXCLUSIVE_FULLSCREEN` for Alt-Tab safety `[UNV]`; any mode or size change fires `Viewport.size_changed` `[BIN]` and is handled as one `RESCALING` frame (C.2) by **one pipeline-root node that is the only writer** of `SubViewport.size`, `size_2d_override` and the camera limits rect (consumed by the solver, C8), and the only caller of `HudLayout` (the HUD & Suit Layer's layout function, which computes the HUD anchors) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from hud-and-suit-layer)* (→ ADR, with PR-7's wiring).

---

**PR-3. The layer model.** One root viewport, one world viewport, and `CanvasLayer`s above it. The question that assigns a thing to a layer is 7.0's: *who produced the information?* — the ship goes in the world and must be lit to read; the suit and the chart table are screen-space and never lit. **The HUD is in a different viewport, not merely a different layer** (engine-programmer): world lights, glow, `CanvasModulate`, the Filter pass on the container and the camera transform cannot reach it by construction, so HR-1, HR-6 and HR-13 are promised structurally. The trade-off is that nothing that must *mix* (a diegetic element lit by the world) may live there — it goes in the world.

| # | Layer (bottom → top) | Where | Scaled by | Lit / glow / ambient / occlusion | Perception Filter | Camera jolt | Contents |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | **World** | `SubViewport` inside a `SubViewportContainer` | k | **yes** — the only layer that is (`use_hdr_2d`, `use_debanding`, filter, snap set on this viewport's own members `[BIN]`; never on the root, which stays SDR-exact for the 4.6 palette) | per-node effects inside; the full-screen pass sits on the container (PR-10) | yes (bounded, PR-6 C7) | floors, floor marks, the Y-sorted layer, caps, lights, occluders, fringe, dust, horrors, web, in-world instruments, the camera |
| 1 | **Transit / load hold** | `CanvasLayer` | — | no | no | no | one opaque black quad, shown only in `TRANSIT_HOLD` and `BOOTING/LOADING` (C.2) |
| 2 | **Suit layer (HUD)** | `CanvasLayer` | H | **no** (HR-1 by construction) | no — readings are fed *perceived values* by the Filter as data; the frame is fed nothing (7.6 hand-off) | **never** (HR-13); prompts use the unjolted transform (C11) | critical strip, suit panel, hand rail, crew strip, interaction prompts, inspect card |
| 3 | **Chart table / menus** | two sibling `CanvasLayer`s with distinct indices (ADR-0008 s2.5): pixel face at layer 3 scale H, reading face at layer 4 scale (1,1) with sizes in screen px | H (bitmap face), continuous (reading face) | no | no | no | pause panel over the frozen world (7.4), web map, settings, text chat |
| 5 | **Debug overlay** | `CanvasLayer`, dev builds only | H | no | **no — it reads the true channel** | no | DIAGNOSTIC surface (C.3); may toggle debug draws *inside* the world viewport (note e) |

Rules that follow: (a) **glow is per viewport** (8.11 flag 3; 4.6 reworked glow to run before tonemapping `[REF]`, so no older tuning transfers and `glow_hdr_threshold` is re-found at 8.12 step 8). (b) The ambient-floor hull material (8.11 flag 1) and the HR-15 low-vision raise are **Lighting's**, applied inside layer 0; the pipeline owns no ambient value and **no full-frame gamma or brightness control exists anywhere** (C-R): the only brightness control the game has is HR-15's bounded hull-material step, which can never reveal web, horrors or filaments and never touches the HUD. (c) In-world instruments are layer-0 lit sprites; the inspect card (7.0) is layer 2 — the same reading drawn twice, once lit and once not. (d) Interaction prompts are layer 2 and are *gated* by the Emitter State exposure query, never by the renderer. (e) Debug draws inside the world (occluder outlines, sort origins, the look-ahead box, the pixel grid, the camera limits) are children of a debug node in layer 0 that exists only in dev builds; `draw_line` there needs explicit width ≥ 1 base px because 4.7 lines have no feather `[REF]`. (f) **NEAREST is set in three places with two differently numbered enums `[BIN]`:** the project setting `rendering/textures/canvas_textures/default_texture_filter = 0` and the world `SubViewport.canvas_item_default_texture_filter = 0` use `Viewport.DEFAULT_CANVAS_ITEM_TEXTURE_FILTER_NEAREST = 0`; the `SubViewportContainer.texture_filter` (a `CanvasItem` member, default `0 = PARENT_NODE`) must be set to `CanvasItem.TEXTURE_FILTER_NEAREST = 1`, or pass A's ×k upscale is bilinear-blurred `[UNV]`. Whether project settings propagate into a `SubViewport` is NOT SOURCEABLE; the startup assertion (PR-15) reads every member back. (g) `SubViewport.size` defaults to 512×512 and `TileSet.tile_size` to 16×16 `[BIN]` — both are set explicitly (640×360 / 48×48); a scene that forgets gets silently wrong output.

*Alternative rejected:* HUD inside the world viewport with `LIGHT_MODE_UNSHADED` materials — unshaded items still receive glow and the full-screen Filter pass, so HR-1 would be a per-node discipline that one forgotten material breaks (8.11 flag 3 made this call; recorded here because the pipeline inherits it).

---

**PR-4. Pixel integrity.** Inside the world viewport:

1. **Nearest sampling everywhere** (`light_` textures excepted — sampled by the light, not the item, 8.4). Mipmaps off on every drawn sheet (8.7).
2. **Integer base-px placement by presentation code — the viewport snap flags are a safety net, not the mechanism** (gameplay-programmer, engine-programmer). Every presentation node computes `pos = floor(prev + (curr − prev) × tick_alpha)` in base px **in the current body frame** and writes that integer; `snap_2d_transforms_to_pixel` and `snap_2d_vertices_to_pixel` are on as belt-and-braces (what they snap to under pass B — base px or render px — is NOT SOURCEABLE; step 12 checks for double-rounding flicker at .5 boundaries). `floor` is the one rounding function used everywhere, including the camera and the beam's chest point (which uses the same rounded position as the sprite, so light origin and sprite never disagree). Because the world canvas *is* the body frame (PR-6 C2), no fractional body offset ever enters an interior position; other bodies drawn in this frame (a docked derelict, a detaching section) are placed by one floored offset shared by all their items, so their items never swim against each other.
3. **Interpolate, then quantise.** Rounding before interpolating produces 1 px stalls; interpolating after rounding is impossible. Consequence to state honestly (engine-programmer): **snap is a floor, not a filter** — interpolation changes only the *timing* of one-base-px steps, never their size. A body moving 1 base px per tick shows a k×k screen block step whenever it moves; the promise is "base-pixel steps at interpolated timing, never twice in a frame at f_render ≥ 60 (below that 1–2 base px per frame at 30–59 fps), never backward", not sub-pixel smoothness *(Amended 2026-10-28 — delegated ruling G4)*. The Player Controller should therefore choose walk and run speeds in **whole base px per tick** when the camera quantum is 1 (PR-6 C3) — routed.
4. **Scale.** Only integer scales, only k (or the zoom step). Horizontal flips are permitted (mirrored facings, 5.6). No per-node scale animation of any kind; `Control.scale` stays `(1,1)` on the HUD. `Camera2D.zoom` stays `(1,1)` **always** (there is no pixel-snap member on `Camera2D` — confirmed by the exhaustive `[BIN]` member list — so zoom is a change of k, PR-6 C6).
5. **Rotation.** `Camera2D.rotation = 0` always, forever (game-designer; also `ignore_rotation = true` default `[BIN]`). Human-built geometry — floors, walls, caps, doors, props, machinery, pipes, seams, characters, tools, overlays, HUD — is **never rotated at runtime** (8.4): a lint asserts `rotation == 0` on every node under the environment, pipe, prop and actor groups. Rotation is permitted for **web pieces** (off-grid by design, 3.3), **horror rig segments** (quantised to 15°, stepped at 12 fps = every 5th tick, 5.4/8.10 — a test), and **lights** (engine light lives off the grid by 8.0's rule). Horror Entities' floor marks (smear, rime, radial hairlines, struck) are **not** web pieces: they are flip-only and never rotated (Horror C-HE-1) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from horror-entities)*. What "rotated segments fall off the grid" means per pass: under pass A a rotated segment is rasterised at base resolution — a stair-step of whole base px, on the grid; under pass B it is rasterised at picture resolution with nearest sampling — angled k×k blocks, same density, **off** the grid. The second is tolerated for horrors and web only (8.4); if it fails review the remedies are 8.10's (24-angle pre-rotated sets, or a base-resolution sub-viewport for horrors alone → ADR). `snap_2d_vertices_to_pixel`'s effect on a rotated quad's corners is NOT SOURCEABLE — step 2 judges the rotating segment. Sub-pixel blur is forbidden in every case.
6. **Half-pixel origins.** `Sprite2D.centered = true` by default `[BIN]`: an odd-dimension texture centred puts its origin on a .5 and wobbles 1 px under NEAREST. 8.2's cells are even (48, 80, 96) and the foot origin (horizontally centred, the top row of the `foot_box`; registry `foot_box`) is set via `offset` *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from ship-body-and-interior-physics)*, so the asset standard satisfies this; the rule for VFX and one-off sprites is **even cells, or `centered = false` with an integer `offset`**.
7. **No sub-pixel sprite positions** means no item is ever drawn at a fractional base-px offset *relative to the grid the other items are on*. Whether the whole grid may translate by screen pixels is the camera quantum's question (PR-6 C3), not this rule's.
8. **One interpolator.** The kernel's `tick_alpha` with previous/current read views is the only interpolator of sim-owned motion: `physics/common/physics_interpolation = false`, the presentation roots are `Node.PHYSICS_INTERPOLATION_MODE_OFF` and children inherit (the post-first-`process_frame` boot walk and the tree-flag test are the checks; ADR-0009 § 5) *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0009 sync)*, `Camera2D.position_smoothing_enabled = false`, `limit_smoothed = false` `[BIN]`. Decisive `[BIN]` reason beyond "two clocks": `get_global_transform_interpolated()` exists on `Node3D` only, so no 2D script could read where an engine-interpolated body is actually drawn — the camera would follow the un-interpolated pose and swim 1–3 render px against the sprite at 144 fps while looking fine at 60. The 2D interpolation machinery does exist in 4.7.2 `[BIN]` (items, lights, occluders); its behaviour is NOT SOURCEABLE and it is simply not used for sim-owned transforms. → ADR (interpolation clock). Whether pure-physics debris may use it is Ship Body's.
9. **Light masks.** `Light2D.range_item_cull_mask` and `CanvasItem.light_mask` default to bit 1 `[BIN]`; the unshaded emissive overlay and the fringe sprites go on a light-mask bit the beams do not light (6.2 "unshaded") — behaviour `[UNV]`, checked at 8.12 step 4. The HUD needs none; it is in another viewport. Bit 3 (decoys) and bits 4 and up (one per live body) are allocated by Lighting (LO-24) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from 2d-lighting-and-occlusion)*.
10. **Lines are sprites.** Hairlines, filaments and seams are sprites, never runtime line draws (8.4, `[REF]`).

---

**PR-5. Projection convention and the draw-order contract (3/4 oblique).** The projection is 1:1 with no foreshortening (8.2): one base px of floor depth, one of width and one of height are each one screen row or column. Floors are 48×48 squares (registry: `TILE_PX`). A wall has a **24 px footprint** on the floor grid, a **72 px front face** and a **24 px top cap**, occupying 96 screen rows but half a tile of floor.

**The convention this contract requires (flagged — blocking for every dependent that sorts).** Art bible 8.2 says the footprint "is drawn as its 24 px top cap, with its 72 px front face hanging below the cap", and separately that "front faces occlude what lies screen-south of their footprint". Taken literally — cap drawn on the footprint rows, face hanging down over the floor to the south — no Y-sort rule can work with a 24-px collision footprint: an actor walking north toward that wall is either drawn floating partway up the face (if sorted by feet against the footprint's south edge) or hidden behind a wall they are physically in front of (if sorted against the face's bottom row), because the face covers 72 rows of *walkable* floor south of the wall. The convention that keeps every number in 8.2 (24 / 72 / 24 / 96, "5.5 tiles of floor with a back wall") and makes sorting consistent is the standard one: **vertical height projects up-screen.** The front face rises from the footprint's south edge (its floor-contact line) 72 rows up-screen; the cap sits above the face. A wall whose footprint is rows `[y0, y0+24)` occupies screen rows `[y0−72, y0+24)` and **occludes what lies screen-north (behind) of its footprint** — the floor of the room behind it, for 1.5 tiles. This changes one clause of 8.2 (screen-south → screen-north) and is **routed to art-director and technical-artist as a spike-room item**: 8.12 step 2 builds a 20×11 room and meets this on day one; the engine-programmer's step-5 test ("a crewmate standing just south of a wall is drawn over its face; just north, behind it") is exactly this convention's acceptance test. *Kit consequence, routed to level-designer:* under this convention a room's own south wall covers 1.5 tiles of the room's floor along its south edge and an actor standing there is hidden — kits need low or cut-away south walls, south-open rooms, or a cap-only south-wall variant, the ordinary 3/4-oblique remedy. If the art-director instead confirms the literal reading, the collision footprint must grow to the full 96 rows from the south (the floor under the face is not walkable), the sort origins below stand unchanged, and 8.2's "only half a tile of floor" and its room arithmetic become false. **Status** *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*: resolved — art bible 8.2 was amended on 2026-10-26 by user decision to "height projects up-screen, so front faces occlude what lies screen-north"; the up-screen convention is now the art bible's, not this GDD's request, and spike step 5's Y-sort room remains its acceptance test (Open Question 2).

*Kit answers for Derelict Authoring (OQ-V7, OQ-V9), given under the up-screen convention above and therefore provisional until Open Question 2 is confirmed at spike step 5* *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from derelict-authoring-handmade-kits)*. **(OQ-V7) Which wall runs bear a front face.** Only runs whose footprint runs east–west bear the 72-row front face, and the face a room's floor shows is the one rising from the south edge of that room's **north (back) wall** (and of any interior east–west run seen from its south side). A north–south run (a side wall) presents its 24-px footprint column and its cap only — a 1:1 projection has no side faces — and a room's own south wall, built as the cap-only or cut-away variant this paragraph requires, bears no visible face. The `PROP_DENSITY_WALL` count therefore runs over the tiles of visible front faces only, never over side walls or cap-only south walls. **(OQ-V9) The free-hanging housing (the EMERGENCY lamp).** It is a sprite on the **wall-caps band (+20, never Y-sorted)**, positioned by its floor anchor and drawn at ceiling height, so its screen rectangle lies 72–96 rows up-screen of the anchor; by the caps-above arithmetic no actor is wrongly overdrawn (an actor south of the anchor never reaches those rows; one north of it is behind it). Because the caps band draws above the floor-marks band, the housing **does occlude any floor trail under its screen rectangle** — not the floor directly beneath its anchor, but the floor 1.5–2 tiles screen-north of it — so Derelict's "at least 1 tile off the coolant floor run" rule must be measured against the housing's drawn screen rectangle, not its anchor.

**Sort origin.** Every object on the Y-sorted layer sorts by its **floor-contact line**: the foot row for actors and horrors (the cell origin of 8.6), the south edge of the footprint for walls, doors, props and machinery nodes — so the wall-face `TileMapLayer.y_sort_origin` `[BIN]` is set to the footprint depth, not 0. Anything **mounted on a wall face** — pipes, fittings, seams, wall gauges, the emissive `_msk` overlay, wall-mounted trails — takes **its host face's sort origin** and draws after the face by tree order. **Ties (equal row) break by a class rank and then by stable entity ID — never by `x`, never by creation order.** The sort key is `(sort_y, class_rank, entity_id)`, with `class_rank` 0 = wall-face tiles < 1 = wall-mounted items < 2 = doors, props, machinery nodes, deployables, empty suits and off-floor web pieces < 3 = actors and horrors, and `entity_id` a **globally unique** persistent int64 allocated by the publisher (the kernel allocates no IDs, kernel CR-8: every ID is block-local, drawn from its owner's counter, Body's `actor_id` from `next_actor_id` (Body BDY-20) included, so for every class, actors and horrors included, `entity_id` is the publisher's namespace-tagged ID, its tag registered with this GDD (Layout OQ-33), e.g. Layout's `entity_id = (LAYOUT_ID_NS << 56) | (section_id << 24) | …`, so IDs from different blocks of one class rank never collide), which survives a save and reload where a creation counter does not; a bare per-block instance ID is **not** accepted as an `entity_id`, because two blocks may both hold instance 3, and the order of two such items would then fall to tree order, which is not save-stable *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review)* *(Amended 2026-10-28 — cross-GDD fix)*. *Why a class rank and not `x`:* an actor standing flush south of a console or door — the commonest interaction posture — has `y_foot == sort_y(prop)`, and an `x` rule draws it over or under the prop depending on which side of the prop's centre it stands, and swaps the order mid-motion when its `x` crosses the prop's `x`; a class rank gives the same answer at every `x` (on an equal row an actor is in front of every wall, door and prop, and walking along the prop changes nothing). *Rejected fallback:* sorting actors by the foot box's bottom row needs Ship Body agreement on BDY-14's top-centre foot origin; it is the remedy only if the spike finds the class rank wrong (Open Question 19). **Mechanism (→ the existing tie-break ADR; `[UNV]`):** Godot's Y-sort has no comparator hook, so the pipeline root keeps the Y-sort node's children in `(class_rank, entity_id)` child order, set at spawn with `move_child` and never re-sorted per frame (an item's class and ID never change), and relies on the engine's tree-order rule for equal `y`; if that rule is not stable, a pipeline-assigned painter's rank written to `z_index` (the world-anchored band mechanism of the fallback below) realises the same key. A fractional sub-row offset is rejected: it collides with PR-4 rule 2 and the PR-15 fractional-transform walk. **Passable footprints.** A door whose Graph opening `area > 0` (a gap exists: an open door, a door in motion, a doorless doorway) is not a wall: its node sorts by the **north edge** of its footprint, `sort_y = y_0`, so an actor whose foot row is inside the gap, and any actor south of it, draws over the frame, lintel and leaf, and an actor north of it is behind them; a closed door (`area = 0`) sorts as a wall, `sort_y = y_0 + 24`. The switch never has an actor inside the footprint, because a door cannot close onto a foot box (Layout LY-11); the same rule applies to the span of a doorway in a north–south run. The debug `actor_in_footprint` check is Body's collision defect only (a foot box that overlaps a SOLID rect, `solid_in`), so a legal doorway crossing never fires it. *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)* Sort origins are **integer base px** — an interpolated actor's sort origin is its floored render row, so an actor and a wall never swap order on a sub-pixel difference.

**The z-band table (PROVISIONAL; exact `z_index` values → ADR, spaced by 10 per the engine-programmer's proposal; all bands must stay inside every `Light2D.range_z_min/max`, default ±1024 `[BIN]`, or lights stop affecting banded items `[UNV]`). *The order of the bands is the contract; the numbers are the ADR's: if the fallback below is built, the Y-sort row becomes a window `[10, 899]` and the cap and dust bands move above it (proposed 900 and 910), so dependents lint band names, never numbers* *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*.**

| Band | z (proposed) | Y-sorted? | Contents | Why here |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Floor | 0 | **never** | floor `TileMapLayer`; grates, drains, floor-level web endpoints | 6.6 rule 4: the floor is the character's ground, not a competitor; batches as ≈ 4 draws (8.6) |
| Floor marks (the trail layer) | +1 | never | **floor trails** in the three sizes, stains, hidden-entity footprints (smear, rime, radial hairlines; Horror's marks are floor-only at MVP — a wall-face mark has no draw band until Vertical Slice, Horror C-HE-1 *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from horror-entities)*), dried puddles | a mark on a floor must never sort above an actor standing on it, and never below the hull it sits on (8.11 flag 1(i)); drawn flat, so order among marks is authoring order |
| Y-sort | +10 | **yes — one `Node2D`** | in tree order: wall-face `TileMapLayer` (`y_sort_origin` = footprint depth); the **wall-mounted layer** — pipes, fittings, seams, wall gauges, the emissive overlay and wall trails, as a second `TileMapLayer`/node set with origins matching their host faces; then doors, props, machinery nodes (96×96 incl. cap), actors with tools and overlays as children, horror rigs, deployables, empty suits, off-floor web pieces, in `(class_rank, entity_id)` child order so equal-row ties resolve without `x` *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)* | the single layer where anything can be in front of anything (8.6). **Pipes must be here, not above the actors**: 8.2 puts the pipe band at rows 29–58 above the floor line, crossing "at head-and-shoulder height" (shoulders ≈ row 37, helmet rows 37–53), so an actor at a wall's base overlaps the band over ≈ 25 rows; an unsorted pipe band above actors would draw pipes over every head along every wall. This moves 8.6's 10–40 pipe draws inside the sort break — the engine-programmer's proposed stack (pipes at +30 above caps) is amended accordingly and the step-5 count changes |
| Wall caps | +20 | never | cap `TileMapLayer` (48×24 slices); free-hanging housings (≤ 24×24, the EMERGENCY lamp — PR-5 kit answers, Derelict OQ-V9) | **draw-call economy, not correctness** (8.6: ≈ 4 draws instead of per-tile sort breaks). Under PR-5's convention the cap's bottom is 72 rows above the footprint's south edge, so an item flush against the wall with face height `f` reaches it iff `f > 72`: **caps-above is safe iff no item's face height exceeds the wall face (72)**. Actors (55) pass; it is a kit rule for props and machinery (→ Edge Cases). If the literal 8.2 reading is confirmed this band becomes load-bearing — one more reason the convention must be settled at the spike |
| Dust & particles | +30 | never | `GPUParticles2D` dust-in-beam, mist, venting | particles are not "in front of" anything in the oblique sense; venting mist above a trail, below nothing |
| Lights & occluders | — | n/a | `PointLight2D`, `LightOccluder2D`; not drawn, so not sorted | occluders attach to the thing that casts (wall footprint, actor), not to a band; a horror casts none — it is solid to Layout and hides what is behind its sprite by draw order, with no occluder and no shadow (Horror C-HE-1) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from horror-entities)* |
| Fringe | — (no band) | n/a | the 2 px violet fringe is a `show_behind_parent` child of its host in the Y-sort band, in the unlit pass (5.5, 8.10) | it inherits its host's order; listed so nobody gives it a band |

**The per-row z-band fallback (8.6).** If the Y-sorted layer exceeds ≈ 300 draws at the spike (8.12 step 5 — "Y-sort breaks quadrant batching" is `[UNV]` and the 50–300 figure NOT SOURCEABLE until then), wall faces and their wall-mounted layer leave the Y-sort node and are drawn by **row band**. *(Rewritten 2026-10-27 — design review: the camera-relative bands of the first draft slid against the world, so "a wall is alone in its band" was false, overlapped the cap and dust bands, and could not be implemented on a `TileMapLayer`, whose `z_index` is per layer, not per cell.)* The bands are **world-anchored and live in a dedicated z window** `[Z_WIN_LO, Z_WIN_HI] = [10, 899]` (the Y-sort row of the table above; caps and dust sit above it): `band(sort_y) = floor(sort_y / BAND_H) − band_0` with `BAND_H = 24` base px (= the wall footprint depth) and `band_0` the first band of the **union rect of every body drawn** (loaded, docked, welding, detaching; recomputed on dock, weld and detach, D.13) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review: was the loaded body's first band, which gave `band < 0` for a docked body above it)*, so an item's band depends only on its body-frame row and never on the camera, and a wall is alone in its band for every camera position. **Mechanism (`[UNV]`, spike step 5):** the wall-face and wall-mounted `TileMapLayer`s are built as **one `TileMapLayer` per band** (each holding the tiles whose `sort_y` falls in that band), parented under a per-band `Node2D` with `z_index = Z_WIN_LO + band` and `y_sort_enabled`, and props, doors, actors and horrors are reparented to the band node of their `sort_y` only when it changes (an integer crossing of a 24-row boundary, at most once per 24 rows moved) with Y-sort and the PR-5 tie key still applying *within* a band node; z values are written on those events, not per frame. The bands needed for the loaded body are `N_bands_body = ceil(body_rows / BAND_H) + 2`, and the bands intersecting the view are `ceil((L_h + 96) / BAND_H) + 1` — the 96 being the cap-over-face overhang, since an item whose `sort_y` is up to 96 rows below the view bottom still draws its cap into view. Nothing visible changes if the mapping is right; the fallback is a cost lever, not a look, and it is **not assumed to save anything**: per-band layers and nodes may add draw calls and CPU rather than remove them, so the spike runs **a go/no-go — draw calls and CPU time with the fallback on must both be lower than with it off in the same room at the largest ladder field; otherwise it is withdrawn and the cost lever is the per-field cap of D.16** *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. **Draw calls are counted per viewport** — `Viewport.get_render_info(RENDER_INFO_TYPE_CANVAS, RENDER_INFO_DRAW_CALLS_IN_FRAME)` `[BIN]` on the world viewport and on the root separately — so the HUD's 20–60 is never confused with the sorted layer's number.

*Alternatives rejected.* Sorting everything (floors included) — floors would then need a sort origin per tile and break batching for no visual gain. A depth buffer / 2.5D approach — not what 2D lighting and occluders expect, and 8.0 says the engine decides how things look, not how they are stacked. Pipes on their own unsorted band — see the Y-sort row.

---

**PR-6. The camera contract.**

**C1. The camera is a window, not a sensor (game-designer, decided).** The camera responds **only to player-owned inputs** — the avatar's position, the beam aim, the player's `zoom_in` / `zoom_out` steps *(Amended 2026-10-26 — consistency check)*, the vehicle the player is in — and **never to world state**: not Attention, breach phase, horrors, sound, the graph, or sanity. The invariant is testable: **an identical input trace produces an identical camera trace in calm, tell and breach** (acceptance test). The camera's framing — position, zoom, rotation, offset — is a **never-shift anchor** in the sense of art bible §1's Corollary: the Perception Filter may not write any camera or viewport property (C-R; the anchor-list addition is routed to the art bible §1 Corollary / 2.9 and to the Perception Filter GDD). The never-list (C9) follows from this.

**C2. Frame: the world canvas is the current body's frame, interior at origin.** The interior scene of the body the avatar is on sits at the origin of the world viewport's canvas; **ship motion through the void never appears in the interior view** — it appears only on exterior or transit layers (`Parallax2D`/`ParallaxBackground` exist `[BIN]`), and **ship rotation is never rendered inside** (gameplay-programmer, engine-programmer option (a); routed to the Ship Body GDD). Consequences: (i) the render rotation of every human-built body is 0 and two docked bodies are both axis-aligned — a hard constraint exported to Ship Body (→ ADR there: `lock_rotation`, or rotate in simulation while presenting in the body frame); (ii) **detached sections translate without rotation in the interior view** (routed to Modular Ship Assembly); (iii) all pixel snapping is in the body frame (C-R), which PR-4 rule 2 already requires; (iv) the pod in transit is its own body — the camera is pod-local and the pod moves at constant velocity against the exterior layer (gameplay-programmer). The follow target is the local seat's controlled entity (kernel binding table, CR-7); **no shared camera in co-op** — every client has its own window. **The camera is a top-level presentation node, never a child of the body** (engine-programmer): `Camera2D.process_callback` is IDLE by default `[BIN]`, a parented camera would inherit the body's transform on the engine's clock and (without `ignore_rotation`) its rotation, and no 2D API exposes an interpolated global transform; the camera script composes the avatar's interpolated body-local position exactly as the actor's presentation node does, with the camera and the Avatar Presenter both calling `Placement.at` on the same `PresentFrame` values, so they cannot disagree regardless of order (ADR-0009 § 3) *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0009 sync)*. If the two compose differently the camera lags the sprite by up to a tick — step 12's zero-render-px-disagreement test. **`Camera2D` applies its scroll in its own internal processing** (`process_callback` IDLE `[BIN]`), so the frame can show last frame's pose against this frame's sprites, and `process_priority` cannot fix that because the engine, not the script, applies the scroll `[UNV]`. The camera host therefore **writes the world viewport's `canvas_transform` directly** from `pose_q` (plus the jolt offset) in a **named hook: the `CameraHost`'s `RenderingServer.frame_pre_draw` handler (the signal that fires after the scene tree's `_process` and before the frame is drawn; ordering probed 2026-10-28 on the 4.7.2 editor and re-run on a release export at spike step 12, release export, Direct3D 12 and the Deck `[UNV]`; the handler does only the two canvas writes, the world `canvas_transform` and the integer parallax offsets, from values the `CAMERA_SOLVE` stage already solved in the single `PipelineRoot._process`, ADR-0008 § 6.2, ADR-0009 § 3; the fallback hook, used only if the signal fails the release-export spike, is the last statement of `PipelineRoot._process` after `driver.frame(...)`, which still precedes the draw)** *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0009 sync)*, after every presentation node has written its position *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review: the earlier "render callback" named no hook)*, so there is no engine scroll to lag; the accepted alternative, if the ADR rejects the direct write, is a `Camera2D` with `anchor_mode = ANCHOR_MODE_FIXED_TOP_LEFT`, drag margins off and `force_update_scroll()` called after each write. Either way **the solver is the only clamp**: `Camera2D.limit_*` stays at the engine defaults (unused), so limits are never clamped twice and `world_to_hud` is never offset while `CLAMPED` *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. **Two variants, every camera assertion dual-pathed; parallax** *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review)*: the **primary variant** writes `canvas_transform` directly and has **no `Camera2D` in the world tree**; the **alternative** is the `Camera2D` of the previous sentence. Every assertion and lint in this GDD that names a `Camera2D` property (`zoom == (1,1)`, `position_smoothing_enabled == false`, `limit_smoothed == false`, `limit_*` at defaults, `process_callback`, the no-physics-parent lint) is read as "holds on the `Camera2D` **if the variant has one**", and each has a primary-variant twin: the world viewport's `canvas_transform` has scale `(1, 1)` and no rotation or skew on every frame, its origin is written by the camera host only, the world tree contains zero `Camera2D` nodes (a lint), and nothing but the host writes `canvas_transform`. **Parallax:** `Parallax2D` / `ParallaxBackground` is assumed to need a `Camera2D` to read the scroll `[UNV]`, so in the primary variant the exterior and transit layers would freeze; and a `scroll_scale` other than 1 gives fractional offsets, which break the grid. The rule is therefore that **the camera host drives every exterior and transit layer itself**: `layer.position = −floor(pose_q · scroll_scale)` rounded to whole base px (so a non-1 `scroll_scale` never gives a fractional offset), written in the same hook as the canvas transform; `Parallax2D` is used only if the spike proves it follows a direct `canvas_transform` write **and** yields whole-base-px offsets, in which case the host drives nothing.

**C3. The solver: a pure function, first-order smoothing, quantised after.** The camera is a `CameraSolver` — a pure `RefCounted` `solve(input, state, params) → pose` with no scene-tree dependency, headless-testable, deterministic, and **frame-rate independent within a stated bound** (gameplay-programmer; → ADR for the node that hosts it): the per-frame factor is exact for a target held constant through the frame, but for a moving target the lag measured after the update, `|target_n − f_n|`, is `v·τ·h/(e^h − 1)` with `h = dt/τ` — 30.9 px at 60 fps, 31.8 px at 144 fps and 29.5 px at 30 fps against the dt→0 limit `v·τ` = 32.4 px at `v_max` (D.6) — so the contract is *the same quantised pose to within 1 base px on a continuous target trace*, never bit-identity, and `v·τ` is the conservative bound every invariant uses (the lag after the update is strictly smaller at every `dt > 0`, so low render rates do not erode the inner-box margin) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. Its follow is a **first-order exponential approach** `cam += (target − cam) × (1 − exp(−dt / τ_follow))` with `τ_follow ≈ 0.18 s` — zero overshoot by construction, so the camera **never reverses direction within 100 ms unless the player did** (C-R). **Settle:** an exponential never arrives, and `floor()` of an asymptotic approach lands one pixel late or never, so every pose term snaps to its target per axis once it is within `SETTLE_EPS = 0.5·Q` (0.5 base px at `Q = 1`): `f → target`, `L → L_target` (D.6, D.7). The solver keeps its state as float64 scalars per axis (never `Vector2`, which is float32 and can stall one ulp short of a target), and the settle makes any residual stall irrelevant. A still scene's pose is therefore constant after a bounded settle and equals `floor(target + L_target)`, including with `L_MAX = 0` (reduced motion); the last pixel of a settle can land up to `t_settle = τ_eff·ln(|d₀| / SETTLE_EPS)` after the avatar stops (≈ 0.75 s from a 32.4 px lag) and is the final step of a motion the avatar caused *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. **No dead-zone** (knob `DEAD_ZONE`, default 0 × 0, kept for A/B). The crewmate is confined to an **inner box ≈ 0.60 of the frame** (half-extents 0.30 × frame = ±192 × ±108 base px about centre at 640×360): follow lag plus look-ahead may never push the avatar outside it. The pose is **quantised after smoothing** to the camera quantum `QUANTUM ∈ {1 base px, 1/k base px}`; **MVP ships on 1** (whole base px in the body frame — C-R), and the `1/k` path — render the world viewport 2 base px larger with the camera at `floor(cam) − (1,1)` and offset the `SubViewportContainer` by `−round((1 + frac(cam)) × k)` screen px, so the picture scrolls in screen-pixel steps while every item stays on one shared base grid — is **built as the upgrade if testers name the stutter** (gameplay-programmer). The `1/k` path costs 2 px overdraw, a container clipped to the picture rect (bars stay untouched), root snap off for the container's fractional root position (shares PR-7's wiring ADR), its pass-B behaviour is `[UNV]`, and it needs **art-director sign-off against 8.0** (the grid translates; its contents do not) **and ux-designer sign-off against C-R "camera moves in whole base px in its body frame"**, which the `1/k` path relaxes to whole screen px. *Systems-designer's recorded risk, for step 12:* with exponential follow and camera and avatar floored independently, the avatar's screen position wobbles ±1 px whenever their fractional parts cross — the one artefact 8.0's grid makes most visible, on the one sprite always on screen. A rigid follow (camera = avatar + offset, smoothing only on the offset) avoids it at the cost of the world scrolling in the avatar's cadence; the A/B is `τ_follow` = 0.18 vs 0 with `DEAD_ZONE` = 0, measured in step 12 as camera-vs-sprite jitter in render px. **Threshold and fallback (step 12):** during a constant-speed walk at `v_walk` and at `v_run`, count the changes of the avatar-to-screen offset `p − pose_q` (base px) from `t_exact` = 10·`τ_eff` = 1.8 s after the run starts from rest (D.6; `3·τ_eff` is only the 95 % time) *(Amended 2026-10-28 — cross-GDD fix)*; the exponential follow ships only if the count is ≤ 2 per second at 60, 90 and 144 fps (PROVISIONAL — the ux-designer ratifies against the footage), and a miss ships **D.6b, the offset-quantised follow** (`pose_q = p + floor(clamp(f − target + L))`: the lag is kept as a constant offset and the camera steps with the sprite, the world scrolling in the avatar's cadence; pure rigid follow is D.6b at `τ_follow` = 0) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review: D.6b is now specified and is the default candidate; the camera-solver story is not ready until D.6b's ACs exist and run)*. The arithmetic predicts a miss above the tick rate: the steady lag `v·τ` is not an integer, so `⌊T⌋ − ⌊T − lag⌋` alternates between two values twice per base px of travel once the render rate exceeds 60 fps (at an integer-speed 60 fps both terms land on integers and the offset is constant) — rigid follow is therefore a likely outcome at 144 fps, not a remote one *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*.

**C4. Look-ahead toward the beam aim.** The frame's half-height is 180 base px = 3.75 tiles against a 6–8-tile beam (8.3): a north-facing wide cone is clipped at under 4 of its 6 tiles, a narrow cone at under half; a south-facing beam runs into the critical strip (7.0's accepted cost). The remedy is a bounded offset **toward the beam aim — not the facing** (game-designer and gameplay-programmer agree), read from the **local raw aim**, not the coalesced intent command (so it carries no tick latency and no quantisation), eased with `τ_lookahead ≈ 0.40 s`, and bounded at `L_MAX = 72` base px = 1.5 tiles (**default**; safe range 1.0–1.56 tiles = 48–75 px at the default field — the 2.5-tile / 120 px bound is unreachable at `v_max` 180, because the inner-box invariant binds at 75.6 px *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, registry alignment)*; the gameplay-programmer argued for 2.0 tiles = 96 px because the vertical clip is the real readability problem and 96 px shows 5.75 of a north beam's 6 tiles — recorded; the game-designer's bound is the one that fits C3's inner box: 72 px look-ahead plus the 32.4 px steady-state follow lag at `v_max` (`v_max·τ_follow` = 180 × 0.18, Player Controller GDD) is 104.4 px, inside the ±108 vertical half-extent, whereas a 96 px look-ahead plus 32.4 px = 128.4 would exceed it; at other ladder steps the look-ahead bound and the solver's other length parameters scale with the visible field, `L_MAX_eff = L_MAX × field_h / 360` (D.7), so the same margin holds at every zoom; the inner-box clamp applies to the final pose while the lag cut (`LAG_CUT` 48 px) reads the unclamped follow term — see the Section D amendment) *(Amended 2026-10-26 — consistency check)*. The offset is quantised with the pose. **Reduced motion disables look-ahead** (`L_MAX → 0`; C-R) — tremor, hairlines and audio carry the information instead. **Open-loop rule (the aim–pose loop is closed by definition, not by test).** Look-ahead reads the aim, so the aim must never read the look-ahead. A mouse aim is the direction from the fixed reference chest `AIM_ORIGIN` to a world point (Player Controller PC-5) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review, delegated ruling R4)*; if that world point came from the full camera pose, which contains `L`, a still mouse would let the lean rotate the aim and the beam toward the existing lean whenever the cursor rests within about `L_MAX` of picture centre — a drift with no cause the player can see (Pillar 1 at the chest-lamp beam, the game's main verb). The aim therefore uses **`mouse_to_world_aim`** (C11, D.14b): the cursor's offset from picture centre in base px, added to the published look-ahead-free follow pose `f_q` — a mapping with no `L`, no inner-box clamp, no jolt and no full camera pose in it, so the aim is a function of the mouse, `k_z` and the follow term (which the avatar's motion alone drives) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review, delegated ruling R4)*. *Trade, stated:* the beam is measured as if the camera sat at the follow pose `f_q` while the drawn avatar sits off-centre by lag plus `L` (up to ≈ 104 px), and while the lag changes the aim point moves with it (`|ΔS| ≤ 36 px`, D.14b) *(Amended 2026-10-28 — cross-GDD fix; G4 recompute)*; with the cursor hidden the player judges by the beam, but the mismatch is a playtest item (Open Question 18). **The lean also scales with aim magnitude** (a smoothstep, D.7) so a cursor crossing the avatar does not swing the frame by `2·L_MAX`, and the slew clamp acts on the vector's length *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. **Run weighting (user ruling 2026-10-27, design-review decision D6 option B)** *(Amended 2026-10-27 — user rulings)*: while the avatar runs, the lean target is weighted toward the direction of movement, so a runner who keeps the beam on a pursuer behind them is not also blinded ahead — the cost of retreating with the beam backward is paid in the beam, not in the player's view of where they are going. The weight `w = LOOKAHEAD_RUN_WEIGHT · r` (D.7) is 0 while walking and its knob value while the Controller's committed gait is a run (eased on screen by `L`'s own ease), reads only the Controller's committed `loco_state`, movement octant and whether the avatar moved on the last 3 ticks (a player-owned input *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review: was the per-tick whole-pixel displacement, which jitters on diagonals)*, so C1 holds: an identical input trace still gives an identical camera trace), never the camera pose, `f`, `L` or the jolt, so the open-loop rule above is untouched; and it is a convex blend, so `|L| ≤ L_MAX_eff` and the inner-box invariant are unchanged. Reduced motion zeroes it with the rest of the look-ahead.

**C5. Pan-speed ceiling and the cut rule (ux-designer, decided).** The camera's pan speed is ceilinged at **1.25 × `v_max`** (the avatar's fastest locomotion; amended from 1.25× walk — see the Section D amendment); if the follow term would exceed it for `T_PAN` = 0.10 s (6 frames at 60 fps), or the follow lag exceeds **`LAG_CUT` = 48 px**, the solver **cuts** (state reset to the target) rather than fast-panning; a **single-frame target jump ≥ 48 base px** (one tile) cuts immediately. These predicates are **backstops**, not tuning for ordinary movement: at MVP no legal locomotion reaches them (a `MAX_TICKS_PER_FRAME` = 4 catch-up delivers at most 4 ticks × 3 px = 12 px, below `JUMP_CUT` and below the 225 px/s ceiling), so they fire only for named sources — a weld `frame_delta_px` that was not rebased, a Body shove or knock that displaces the avatar without a walk, hitch recovery (`dt` beyond `DT_MAX` followed by a burst), a restore, or a zoom-step rescale that were ever skipped — and the fixtures that exercise them are explicitly synthetic. The camera **teleports (full solver state reset to the target)** on every `LOADING → RUNNING` and every pod or transit body transfer (gameplay-programmer, ux-designer); a weld or detach `BODY_TRANSFER` that carries a `frame_delta_px` is instead a **rebase**: the solver shifts `f` and its history by `frame_delta_px` into the new body frame and **keeps the lag and `L`**, so the avatar's screen position does not move (zeroing the lag shifted the avatar by up to 32 px), and only a transfer whose hull jump cannot be guaranteed zero (BDY-27, Edge Cases) is hidden by a hard cut, never a pan. After a `LOADING → RUNNING` or a wake the solver starts with **`L = 0` and eases it in** at the slew rate, so the first frame is `pose_q = quantise(target)` whatever the aim was at the instant of the cut and re-wake framing is reproducible; a cut the solver triggers itself (`P_pan`, `P_lag`, `P_jump`) resets `f` and keeps `L`, because the lean is player-caused *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. **Camera state is never persisted**; after a restore the solver starts from the target. Because the solver interpolates from previous/current, the kernel calls `snap_baseline` on every interpolated block on each transition into `RUNNING`, so the first frame after a load or wake interpolates nothing from a stale pose; that is the only restore-continuity mechanism and no owner-side `post_restore` rule exists (ADR-0008 § 6.1, ADR-0001 § 4, TD-22). The camera stage additionally CUTs the solver in `on_discontinuity(DISCONTINUITY_RESTORE)`, which the presentation driver delivers when `PresentationClock.load_seq` changes (the kernel increments it on every `LOADING → RUNNING`, never on `PAUSED` or `SAVING → RUNNING`) *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0008 sync)*.

**C6. Zoom: integer ladder in both directions, persistent, cut not tween.** `Camera2D.zoom` stays `(1,1)`; "zoom" is a change of the world's integer scale: press `zoom_in` / `zoom_out` (edge-triggered; one step per press; persistent — never held — and reset to default on re-wake) to step `k` through the integer ladder `[1, k_base + 2]` (user decision 2026-10-26, supersedes the game-designer's single held step out); it is available in every state including breach (it is a player input, C1). At 1080p the ladder is 1×, 2×, **3× default**, 4×, 5× (sprites at 33 / 67 / 100 / 133 / 167 %); 8.1's "one integer step out" is the first `zoom_out` press (amendment routed to art-director). C-R's "no zoom-out below a 2× world" is **superseded**: on a 2× world (720p, Steam Deck, manual 2×) the ladder reaches 1× (decided 2026-10-26, creative director concurs, user ratification pending — Open Question 4; on-device acuity check routed to the accessibility-specialist). The logical size at a ladder step `k_z` is `picture / k_z`, **uncapped** (PR-2's cap applies to default zoom only; at 1440p (`k_base` = 4) one step out is 852×480 logical (`k_z` = 3; 1280×720 is two steps out, `k_z` = 2), and letterboxing it would defeat the step) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review: 1280×720 is not one step out at 1440p)*; at 1080p one step out is 960×540 = 20 × 11.25 tiles, so the 8 × 6 machine room of 8.2 fits, and 1× is 1920×1080 base = 40 × 22.5 tiles. The solver's length parameters scale with the field (`s = field_h / 360`, D.6–D.10) so the camera feels the same at every step. The change is a **cut**: one frame at the old k, the next at the new, no intermediate scale (none is integer), no zoom pulse. The HUD does not change (PR-12). Zoom-in **is** offered as integer steps up to `k_base + 2` (user decision 2026-10-26 — see the zoom-ladder note; the tight default frame remains the default, not the only frame). Every step is implemented by changing the world viewport's **logical size** (pass A: `size`; pass B: `size_2d_override`), never `Camera2D.zoom` — which shares the unresolved wiring question of PR-7 (two integer scales in one window under one root stretch) and is folded into spike step 11. **"Across the room" readability** (Pillar 2's frosting elbow seen to worsen from across the room) is satisfied by trail propagation into frame, look-ahead, wide-and-shallow kits (8.2) and the player's zoom steps — **never by a wider default frame** (game-designer). *Playtest metric:* the share of boarding time spent away from default zoom, in either direction; **> 50 % → revisit the default frame** (routed to game-designer and the playtest plan). *(Amended 2026-10-26 — consistency check: held-step wording replaced by the edge-triggered ladder throughout.)* **Input pacing.** A wheel burst can deliver dozens of notches in one frame, so the pipeline root accepts **at most one step per frame and at least `ZOOM_STEP_MIN_INTERVAL` = 0.15 s between accepted steps**, and discards the surplus (never queued; `diag.zoom_step_rate_limited` counts them); a button press is subject to the same interval. **`k_base` rule.** The ladder is built on `k_base` — the manual `k` when Settings sets one below Auto, else `k_auto` — and `k_base` is the default step, the `k` of the pinned HUD rect's picture (and **not** of `H_default`, which stays `max(k_auto, 3)`: a manual smaller world scale never shrinks the HUD); with a manual `k` the aspect is `keep` (PR-2), so a manual-2× player at 1080p has the ladder 1×–4× and the default frame of a 2× player, not a permanently wider one. **MVP death stub.** At MVP the failure state is the death stub — restart from the last autosave (user decision) — an in-session `LOADING → RUNNING` restore in place: the camera `CUT`s at the **pre-load `k_z`** (zoom is pipeline state and is not reset; only the VS-A re-wake resets it, C10), with `L` starting at 0 and easing in *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*.

**C7. Jolt (shake): a bounded knob, default 0.** No camera shake in MVP (game-designer). A **physical-jolt knob** exists for VS-A revisiting: an integer base-px offset of at most `JOLT_MAX = 2`, bounded duration, applied **as a camera offset inside the world `SubViewport` — `Camera2D.offset`, or added to the directly written canvas transform (C2) — never as a container offset** (C-R — so bars and HUD are untouched by construction); caused **only by P1 physical events** the player's body is subject to (hull section separation, docking contact — Web Map & Transit's `BERTHED` for a berth, Body's `BODY_DOCK_CONTACT` for a weld — a detach (the single trigger list; hull impact and vent bursts are deferred to the VS-A decision, Open Question 14)) and **never by Attention, Sanity, the Tell or breach** (C1; 2.9: sanity is never read off the screen's energy; 2.8: breach is more things moving, not the screen fighting the player). Default 0 at MVP; **reduced motion sets it to 0** regardless. *Berth Settle profile (ASK-AD-6; registry `BERTH_SETTLE_PX`, Web Map & Transit's value)* *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from web-map-and-transit)*: on `BERTHED` the jolt API applies one **Settle** instead of D.11's alternation — a single displacement of at most `BERTH_SETTLE_PX` (2 art px, never above `JOLT_MAX`) along the socket axis, damped to rest in 250–400 ms with ≤ 5 % overshoot and no 6–10 Hz component, at half amplitude on the release; presentation only, applied inside the world `SubViewport`, excluded from `world_to_hud`, 0 under reduced motion (the creative-director's single-displacement preference of Open Question 14). It inherits this rule's MVP gate: while the jolt knob is 0 at MVP the Settle is not rendered; the berth Settle is held to VS-A with Open Question 14 (Resolved 2026-10-28 — delegated ruling R21, Open Question 16) *(Amended 2026-10-28 — delegated ruling R21)*. World-anchored HUD prompts use the **unjolted** camera transform (C11).

**C8. Limits.** With the world in body-local coordinates the limits are the loaded body's bounding rect (`int` base px), **set by a `PROJECTOR` when sections weld or detach** (kernel CR-10 class), never by the camera itself, and **consumed by the solver alone**: the solver clamps `c` per axis to `[rect.min + field/2, rect.max − field/2]` (centred on the rect on an axis where the field is larger than the rect), and `Camera2D.limit_*` is left at the engine defaults (±10 000 000, unused) so the engine never clamps a second time — a double clamp would offset `world_to_hud` while the camera is `CLAMPED` (C2) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*. No authored rect means no clamp, and the void beyond a hull is honest black. When an authored rect clamps the pose on an axis the camera is `CLAMPED` (C.2). The clamp's formula and its order against the inner box are D.8b: inner box first, limits second and winning; a changed rect is eased (`rect_eff`), never popped, except on a cut *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review)*. Bounds are the only "fixed frame" the pipeline offers; there is **no cinematic takeover** of any kind.

**C9. Never on the world camera** (game-designer, ux-designer): rotation; non-integer zoom; a zoom pulse or "FOV punch"; blur; chromatic aberration; vignette; uncaused drift; free-look or peek; auto-aim or any aim assist expressed through framing; a revealing pan; a cinematic cut or hold; a minimap or edge marker (prompts anchored to objects **never clamp to the frame edge** — an off-screen object has no prompt); a shared camera in co-op; any write by the Perception Filter or by Attention/Horror/Sanity presentation.

**C10. Transit, crew loss, re-wake.**
- *Pod transit.* The hop is a one-tick data change followed by a LEG (exterior chase, pod-local, constant velocity) and an `ARRIVING` phase in which a `PROJECTOR` builds the destination (kernel C.3 #8) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from web-map-and-transit)*. Hop `Applied` → pipeline enters `TRANSIT_HOLD` (layer 1 black, HUD stays — HR-3) → if Web Map & Transit presents an exterior leg (2.5), **cut** to the pod body and follow it with the ordinary solver, pod-local, constant velocity — **no cinematic cuts within the leg** (game-designer: "the camera follows the pod exactly as the crewmate") → black → destination ready (`web.view.destination_ready` and the pipeline's own projector latch both hold) → **cut** to the avatar on the derelict. The two hop boundaries are body transfers and therefore cuts (C5) through black; the pipeline never fades the *world's light* — a world fade is a mask (Principle 1); the plate is above the world and below the HUD, and it is the void. Fade to black `120 ms` out, cut in (PROVISIONAL, 7.3's menu-fade range; game-designer and art-director own the feel). The hold plate is `#000000` and carries nothing — no text, spinner or tip (ASK-AD-6). On a restore (`LOADING → RUNNING`) into `ARRIVING` the pipeline re-enters `TRANSIT_HOLD` while `web.view.transit_state == ARRIVING` (black until `destination_ready`); on a restore into LEG it presents the exterior leg (Web Map & Transit E-25). **Exterior-body draw rule (WM-19):** interior scene content is drawn only for the avatar's body and bodies docked or welded to it; the leg target's unlit exterior silhouette and residual-lamp point (WM-18) are exempt *(Amended 2026-10-27 — consistency pass, routed from web-map-and-transit)*.
- *Breach.* **No camera change** of any kind (C1).
- *Crew loss and re-wake.* 2.10's sources going out one by one is Lighting reading state; the camera **holds exactly where it was until the hard cut** (no drift, no pull-in); on `LOADING` the pipeline shows layer 1 black; on `RUNNING` it cuts to the new avatar under the cryo-bay lamp with **framing identical to the first wake — snap, not ease** (game-designer; 2.2: "I know this room" before "something has changed"); `L` starts at 0 and eases in (C5), so the framing is reproducible whatever the aim was. **MVP:** the failure state is the death stub, a restart from the last autosave — an in-session restore in place (`LOADING → RUNNING`, a hard `CUT` at the pre-load `k_z`, C6), not the VS-A re-wake; it has no lights-out beat and does not reset zoom *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*.

**C11. `world_to_hud(pos)`.** The pipeline publishes one projection from body-space position to HUD-layer coordinates — accounting for k, H, the container offset of the `1/k` quantum if active, letterboxing and zoom, using the **unjolted** camera pose — quantised to the HUD's own integer grid. Interaction prompts, the inspect card's anchor and any future ping call it; nobody re-derives the projection. Mouse-to-world (`SubViewportContainer.mouse_target`, `Viewport.get_mouse_position()` `[BIN]`, which, with root stretch disabled (wiring (i), ADR-0008 § 3.3), is already physical window px — the pipeline reads the cursor as **physical window px** (no inverse `get_final_transform` is needed) before any division by `k_z`, D.14b) *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0008 sync)* goes through the same node, is checked at step 11, and is published as **two** mappings because one of them sits inside the aim loop *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*: (a) **`mouse_to_world_view(cursor)`** — the full unjolted camera pose including `L`, the inverse of `world_to_hud`'s world half, for picking, pings and anything asking what lies under the cursor on screen; **nothing that feeds the beam aim may call it** (lint); (b) **`mouse_to_world_aim(cursor, p)`** — open loop, D.14b: `f_q + (cursor − picture_centre) / k_z`, where `f_q` is the **published** look-ahead-free follow pose (D.6 `f` floored to `Q`), with no `L`, no inner-box clamp, no jolt and no pose; the pipeline publishes `f_q`, `picture_centre` and `k_z`, and the cursor is read in physical window px (D.14b). Player Controller PC-5's mouse aim already reads (b) in this form (Open Question 18), so the look-ahead, which the aim drives, can never feed back into the aim *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review, delegated ruling R4)*. The pipeline root's input mapping must not test `device == 0` (4.7: `DEVICE_ID_MOUSE/KEYBOARD` `[REF]`).

---

**PR-7. Render mode: pass A or pass B, one flag, decided by the spike — and the one wiring question three features share.** The two passes are one scene (8.4): **A** renders the world viewport at logical size (640×360 texels; lit pixels 230 400) and the container scales it by k — lights, shadows and glow at base resolution, cone and shadow edges stair-stepped by one base px; **B** renders the world viewport at the picture size (`logical size x k_z`, never the window, so the override divides it exactly; probed 2026-10-28: a 1366x768 viewport over a 682x384 override gives a stretch of 2.0029, not an integer) with `size_2d_override = logical size` and `size_2d_override_stretch = true` `[BIN]` (ADR-0008 s4) *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0008 sync)* — canvas coordinates logically 640×360, sprites integer-scaled **only if item origins land on multiples of k render px** (PR-4 rule 2 guarantees this for sim-driven motion; tile layers and the camera are checked at step 2/12), lights, shadows and glow at picture resolution (`logical size × k_z`; 2 073 600 lit px at 1080p — the 9× lever of 8.11 flag 4). 8.12 step 2 decides on the criteria written there (B wins only inside 16.6 ms with 8.10's budget); **A is the fallback the art-director accepts and the default until the spike runs.** Recorded as `RENDER_MODE ∈ {A, B}` → ADR.

**The wiring question (engine-programmer; NOT SOURCEABLE; step 11).** Under one root `canvas_items` + `integer` stretch the container is 640×360 base units and `stretch = true` plausibly sizes the `SubViewport` to 640×360 (= pass A); `stretch_shrink` only shrinks `[BIN]`. How a world viewport reaches W×H (pass B), or `picture / k_z` (any ladder step, C6), or how the HUD draws at H ≠ k (PR-12 — mandatory on the Deck from MVP), while the root stays in integer stretch, is not derivable from the members. Candidate wirings: **(i)** root `stretch/mode = disabled`, world `SubViewport.size` set by the pipeline root on `size_changed`, container placed and scaled by hand, HUD `CanvasLayer.scale = H` set by hand; **(ii)** root integer stretch kept, container `stretch = false`, `SubViewport.size` driven by script, relying on the final transform to map base units onto render px 1:1; **(iii)** root integer stretch kept and the `SubViewportContainer.stretch = true` with `stretch_shrink` = `k` (the container renders its viewport at `1/stretch_shrink` of its size, so a 640×360-unit container at root scale `k` hosts a viewport of `(640, 360) · k / stretch_shrink` px) — **evaluated here, not adopted**: it is the only candidate that keeps the root stretch and needs no script-set scale, but `stretch_shrink` is an integer divisor `[BIN]` and the ladder steps need a viewport of `picture / k_z`, which is not `k / integer`, so (iii) can serve the default step and pass B at `H = k` only; the ADR records it as the fallback if (i) and (ii) both fail the readback-chain criteria (ADR-0008 § 5.4) *(Amended 2026-10-27 — re-review)*. **Pass B, zoom-out and HUD ≠ k are one ADR**, not three; the art bible's "both passes are one scene" glosses this. Whichever wins, the acceptance test is the mode-independent readback chain of ADR-0008 s5.4: world effective scale = root `get_final_transform` scale x `WorldContainer.get_global_transform` scale x `WorldViewport.get_stretch_transform` scale, exactly `(k_z, k_z)`; HUD effective scale = root `get_final_transform` scale x `HudLayer.transform` scale, exactly `(H, H)`, at every window in PR-1's table (a SubViewport's own `get_final_transform` is identity under pass A, so the literal wording measures different things in A and B). *(Amended 2026-10-29 — ADR-0008 sync: wiring (i) is the ADR's decision, § 2; (ii) and (iii) stay recorded as alternatives)* **Leading candidate: (i)** *(Amended 2026-10-27 — design review)*.
